where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm91" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm92" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> are two cipher images corresponding to two plain images differing by one single pixel. The range of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm93" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm94" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> and the range of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm95" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm96" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<p>The mean NPCR and UACI results for our encryption algorithm are listed in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>. For comparison, the NPCR and UACI values for the cipher images generated by the two encryption schemes described in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>] and Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>] are also included in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>. The encryption scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>] uses key generation machines that rely on ordinary images. From the analysis of experimental results, all encryption schemes have defense capabilities against differential attacks.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/entropy-27-00838-t004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec4dot1dot4-entropy-27-00838"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.4. Statistical Attack</h4>
<p>Correlation analysis involves examining the relationship between two or more variables to assess their degree of correlation. In the context of images, adjacent pixels typically exhibit high correlation, meaning that a pixel often contains information about its neighboring pixels. Attackers can exploit this by analyzing specific data segments between plaintext and ciphertext images, attempting to predict the plaintext without knowledge of the encryption key. As a result, image encryption schemes must effectively reduce the correlation between image pixels. In <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> and <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>, we measure correlation in three directions: horizontal, vertical, and diagonal. The correlation coefficients for various images are provided, with the Quality Factor (QF) set to 50.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="entropy-27-00838-t005"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 5.</h5>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/entropy-27-00838-t006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>In <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>, the correlation between adjacent pixels in the image encrypted using our algorithm is significantly lower than other methods. This reduction is attributed to the added entropy encoding stage in our encryption process. When compared to the three schemes, our algorithm effectively diminishes the correlation between adjacent pixels, particularly in the vertical direction. <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t007" class="usa-link">Table 7</a> presents the correlation values for the original ‘Mandrill’ image and the ciphertexts produced by various encryption schemes.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/entropy-27-00838-t007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec4dot1dot5-entropy-27-00838"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.5. Information Entropy Analysis</h4>
<p>Information entropy is an important metric used to quantify the randomness of the pixels in the images [<a href="#B48-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-entropy-27-00838">48</a>]. Information entropy is close to eight after encrypted gray-image mapping the effectiveness of the proposed encryption algorithm. The results of information entropies with different algorithms in distinct images are exhibited in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-t008" class="usa-link">Table 8</a>, in which the information entropy of our presented encryption scheme is higher than Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>]. Moreover, the result of the proposed algorithm is close to, and slightly higher than, Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], which has proven impressive for our algorithm in terms of ciphertext attacks to some extent.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/entropy-27-00838-t008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="sec4dot2-entropy-27-00838"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Compression Performance</h3>
<p>Our encryption scheme is integrated into the lossy JPEG compression process, with compression performance influenced by the Quality Factor (QF) values. A higher QF value results in less compression, leading to a larger bitstream size. To evaluate compression performance, we use the bits per pixel (BPP) metric. The average BPP values are calculated for the reconstructed images. The experimental images used are the nine shown in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>. In <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>, we present the QF-BPP curves for various encryption schemes, with QF values ranging from 10 to 90. From the average QF-BPP curves shown in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>, the compression performance of our proposed scheme is better than Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>] and the second scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], but worse than the first scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>]. This can be attributed to the introduction of the RSV (Replaced and Reassembled Variable) in our approach, which increases the bitstream size. Unlike the first scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], our encryption does not occur during the entropy encoding stage. In contrast, the second scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>] adds an additional RSV pair for each block during the entropy encoding encryption process, along with an EOB (End-of-Bit) embedding step, resulting in a larger bitstream size. However, our scheme only incorporates half of the additional RSV pairs, leading to better compression performance compared to the second scheme in Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>]. Moreover, the change in compression savings (measured as BPP) against image quality (measured as PSNR and SSIM) is plotted in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>. As BPP increases, the proposed strategy generates higher-quality compressed images than JPEG, Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], and Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="entropy-27-00838-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12385766_entropy-27-00838-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cd26/12385766/4012068d2fa1/entropy-27-00838-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="483" width="714" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/entropy-27-00838-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The QF-BPP curves with different encryption schemes: nine test images (Proposed Algorithm, Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], and Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>]).</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="entropy-27-00838-f010"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 10.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12385766_entropy-27-00838-g010.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cd26/12385766/bccc0f789b87/entropy-27-00838-g010.jpg" loading="lazy" height="380" width="764" alt="Figure 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/entropy-27-00838-f010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The PSNR-BPP-SSIM curves with different compression schemes(Proposed Algorithm, Ref. [<a href="#B18-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-entropy-27-00838">18</a>], and Ref. [<a href="#B31-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-entropy-27-00838">31</a>]).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec4dot3-entropy-27-00838"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3. Computational Complexity Analysis</h3>
<p>A promising algorithm obeys low time complexity; we combined JPEG compression with encryption to balance data security and compression efficiency in this study. The time complexity of this algorithm mainly includes three parts: compression, key generation, and encryption. Four steps occur in the JPEG compression algorithm that has been presented thoroughly in <a href="#sec2dot1-entropy-27-00838" class="usa-link">Section 2.1</a>, and the computation complexity of JPEG compression is approximately O(<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm97" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>), where <em>M</em> and <em>N</em> represent the image’s size. The pseudo-random sequences are generated by the Lorenz hyperchaotic system, in which the time complexity is O(<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm98" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>), wherein n expresses the iterated times of the chaotic system. The encryption algorithm mainly contains five stages: coefficient distribution, block permutation, DC coefficient encryption, non-zero AC coefficient sign transformation, and RSV pair separate–permute–restore–permute, in which the time complexity is O(<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm99" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>64</mn></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>+</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></span>), where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm100" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the number of DC coefficients, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm101" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> signifies the number of AC coefficients, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm102" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> stands for the number of non-zero 8<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm103" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo> </mo><mo>×</mo><mo> </mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>8 blocks, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm104" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> indicates the number of RSV pairs in every 8<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm105" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo> </mo><mo>×</mo><mo> </mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>8 block. Therefore, the computational complexity of this algorithm is O(<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm106" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>64</mn></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>+</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>+</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></span>), which after simplifying is approximately O(<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm107" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>M</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mi>s</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>).</p></section><section id="sec4dot4-entropy-27-00838"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.4. The Effect of Encryption Strategy on Compression</h3>
<p>This section presents a detailed analysis of the effect of different encryption methods on compression performance, including file size and execution time. The compression performance should be fully accounted for in the stage of designing the encryption algorithm.</p>
<section id="sec4dot4dot1-entropy-27-00838"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.4.1. The Effect of DCT Permutation on Compression</h4>
<p>A coefficient permutation strategy based on the generated key is proposed in the DCT transformation phase. A comprehensive analysis is presented in this subsection on the effect of this encryption method on compression, using the common image (Mandrill) as an example to test the variation of bit increase and time overhead with different QF values, which are plotted in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a> and <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>. Obviously, the file size and computational overhead are enhanced as QF increases. Among them, the blue solid is the compression baseline, and the green solid expresses the result of coefficient permutation. Experimental results have demonstrated that the proposed scheme incurs a slight increase in bit size and computational time.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="entropy-27-00838-f011"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 11.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12385766_entropy-27-00838-g011.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cd26/12385766/0c983b307252/entropy-27-00838-g011.jpg" loading="lazy" height="497" width="780" alt="Figure 11"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/entropy-27-00838-f011/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The variation of file size.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="entropy-27-00838-f012"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 12.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12385766_entropy-27-00838-g012.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cd26/12385766/0c84ef97a51b/entropy-27-00838-g012.jpg" loading="lazy" height="379" width="653" alt="Figure 12"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/entropy-27-00838-f012/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The variation of computational time.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec4dot4dot2-entropy-27-00838"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.4.2. The Effect of Shuffling on the DPCM</h4>
<p>The block permutation and DC encryption are performed in this study, which may affect the next step. In this subsection, we prove the algorithm’s feasibility using the Mandrill image. As depicted in <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a> and <a href="#entropy-27-00838-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>, the red solid quantizes this encryption result, and all values are basically similar to the compression result that represents the blue solid. The results indicate that the encryption algorithm demonstrates efficacy despite its negligible impact on deposit space and communication costs.</p></section></section></section><section id="sec5-entropy-27-00838"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusions</h2>
<p>In this study, we propose a novel scheme that combines compression and encryption. We begin by designing a block splitting method that divides each 16 × 16 block into four 8 × 8 blocks, based on the 16 × 16 Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT), to ensure compatibility with the standard quantization table of the JPEG compression standard. Next, block permutation is applied to disrupt the correlation between blocks. The DC coefficient and nonzero AC coefficients are encrypted using pseudo-random key sequences generated by the Lorenz hyperchaotic system. Additionally, we introduce RSV pair recombination during entropy coding to further break the correlation between adjacent pixels, thereby enhancing data security. All RSV pairs are split into two parts within the blocks. Each block in the first part is paired with a corresponding block in the second part, and the RSV pairs between these two blocks are permuted and recombined. Moreover, an extra RSV pair, representing the displacement of the segmentation point, is embedded within each block of the first part. Compared to existing schemes, only half of the additional RSV pairs are embedded while enhancing the data security and running efficiency. Extensive experiments and various evaluation metrics show that our approach significantly improves compression efficiency and noticeably ruins the pixel correlation to immunize against the various attacks. However, the vulnerability of our algorithm did not fully disrupt intra-block pixel correlations, resulting in low information entropy in ciphertext images. In future work, we will address this limitation by increasing, reducing, or modifying a part of the pixel to break the intra-block pixel correlations.</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
